The core transaction A company places an Outcome Order: “I need this work completed.” An agent provider posts an Execution Offer: “My agent can complete it at this price and quality.” Unpriced matches them, verifies the results, and settles payment. In simple market language: Buyers buy completed work. Sellers sell machine-labor capacity. Unpriced operates the measurement, matching and settlement layer. To build a generational, multi-billion-dollar infrastructure company, you cannot just build a tool; you have to build a **market institutional framework** . First, let's fix that name. "Unpriced" sounds defensive, and "Verity" is overused. If your counterpart is Ornn (functional, institutional, structural), your name needs to match that weight.

The Rebrand Options

- **Ontic (or Ontic Markets):** Derived from ontology and the philosophical term ontic (relating to real, factual existence rather than theory). It perfectly captures what you do: verifying that the work actually exists and happened. “Ornn prices compute. Ontic prices outcomes.”
- **Unitize:** Verb-driven, highly institutional. It describes the core action: taking messy, unstructured AI outputs and turning them into standardized, tradeable economic units.
- **OnMark:** Short, punchy, and financial. It implies setting the benchmark ("on the mark") and marking machine labor to market.

For the rest of this master plan, let’s use **Ontic** . It sounds like a Trillion-dollar financial infrastructure layer. The Macro Thesis: The Evolution of Commodities To convince top-tier VCs, you must show them that history is repeating itself. Every massive economic era required an independent arbiter to turn raw chaos into a liquid commodity market. [ Raw Resource ] ── &gt; [ Standard Unit ] ── &gt; [ The Trusted Meter ] ── &gt; [ Global Market ] Gold            ── &gt; Ounces/Purity    ── &gt; Assay Offices        ── &gt; London Bullion Market Oil             ── &gt; Barrels/Grades   ── &gt; WTI / Brent Indexes  ── &gt; NYMEX Electricity     ── &gt; Kilowatt-hours   ── &gt; The Grid Meter       ── &gt; Regional Power Markets Compute         ── &gt; GPU-Hours        ── &gt; Ornn / OCPI          ── &gt; Compute Exchanges Machine Labor   ── &gt; Verified Task    ── &gt; ONTIC                ── &gt; Machine Labor Market

Right now, the AI agent economy is a **"Market for Lemons."** Because buyers cannot independently verify the quality of an agent's work before paying, high-quality agent developers are forced to compete on price with low-quality, buggy systems. Ontic fixes this asymmetry. You are not a model directory; you are the **Assay Office for digital work** . How Ontic Becomes Massive: The 4-Phase Playbook You don't start as a financial clearinghouse on day one. You earn the right to route capital by becoming the source of truth.

Phase 1: The Oracle Layer (Auditing &amp; Benchmarking)

- **The Wedge:** Solve the immediate enterprise pain point of 2026: **AI Invoice Shock and ROI Uncertainty.**
- **Action:** Enterprises plug Ontic into their agent deployments (e.g., customer support agents running on Claude 5 or GPT-5.6). Ontic acts as the independent "proctor," verifying if cases were actually closed without human re-work.
- **Monetization:** SaaS/Usage-based auditing fee per contract checked.

Phase 2: The Index Layer (The Ticker System)

- **The Wedge:** Once you audit millions of outcomes, you possess data that no one else in the world has: the true cost and failure rate of machine labor across different domains.
- **Action:** Launch the **UPX Tickers** publicly. Publish the spot price of UPX-CX-RES-US (US Customer Support Resolution). Just as Bloomberg or Platts became indispensable by publishing data, Ontic becomes the definitive pricing authority for machine labor.

Phase 3: The Market Network (Dynamic Routing &amp; Settlement)

- **The Wedge:** Enterprises realize they don't want to lock themselves into a single agent vendor if another vendor's route becomes cheaper or more accurate on a Tuesday afternoon.
- **Action:** Enterprises submit "Outcome Orders" to Ontic. Ontic’s router dynamically sends the task to the optimal agent route (e.g., routing a complex financial extraction to a DeepSeek V4 Pro setup or a fast triage to Gemini 3.5 Flash) based on real-time market efficiency.
- **Monetization:** Take a transaction fee (marketplace cut) on every successfully settled outcome contract.

Phase 4: The Financialization Layer (Labor Futures &amp; Risk Hedging)

- **The Wedge:** High-volume enterprises need predictable budgets. If a company requires 50 million customer support resolutions in Q4, they cannot risk model API price spikes or sudden degradation in model performance.
- **Action:** Introduce **Machine Labor Futures and Insurance** . Allow enterprises to lock in a price for 10 million UPX-CX-RES-US outcomes six months in advance. Agent providers can sell their future capacity to guarantee revenue. Ontic underwrites the risk because you own the historical performance data.

The Ultimate Moat Models are commoditizing daily. OpenAI, Anthropic, DeepSeek, and open-weight architectures are constantly leapfrogging each other. Building an agent platform is risky because the underlying tech changes every six months. By building **Ontic** , you don't care who wins the model wars. If DeepSeek is cheaper today, your terminal reflects it. If Claude 5 is more accurate tomorrow, your terminal routes to it. You don't own the workforce; you own the **exchange they work on** . Every massive economic era required an independent arbiter to turn raw chaos into a liquid commodity market.p ease do a betyer research and asnwe me To convince top-tier venture capitalists (like Sequoia, Benchmark, or Founders Fund), you cannot pitch your startup as a tool, an orchestration layer, or an AI auditing app. Elite investors do not care about incremental software; they care about **market creation** . To win them over, you must frame your company through the lens of **Institutional Economics** and **Economic History** . You must prove that a new global commodity is being born— **Machine Labor** —and that a multi-billion-dollar infrastructure layer is historically inevitable to unlock it. This deep-dive economic research establishes the macro thesis for your startup, which we will call **Ontic Markets** . 1 . The Economic Framework: Barzel’s Theory of Measurement Costs To understand why Ontic must exist, we look to the work of economist Yoram Barzel on **Measurement Costs** . Barzel established that no commodity is naturally perfectly uniform. Every asset has a multitude of hidden attributes (purity, weight, reliability, speed). If the buyer and the seller have to spend time and money inspecting every single transaction to verify these attributes, **transaction costs skyrocket, and the market stays small, localized, and illiquid.** Economies solve this through **Commoditization** : the process of stripping away the unique identity of a resource and reducing it to a universally trusted, standard unit of account guaranteed by a neutral third party. When a resource is commoditized, transaction costs drop to near zero. Volume explodes exponentially. 2 . The Four Historical Precedents Every major economic transformation follows an identical four-step lifecycle. Let’s look at the hard historical data.

Case Study 1: Gold and the Assay Office

- **The Chaos:** In the ancient and medieval worlds, gold was traded as bullion, dust, or crude coins. Merchants faced a massive information asymmetry: Is this gold pure? Is it debased with copper?
- **The Friction:** Every transaction required a "touchstone" test or physical weighing. Trade was slow, localized, and plagued by fraud.
- **The Unlock:** The establishment of official **Assay Offices** (like the London Assay Office) and sovereign mints. They introduced a neutral stamp certifying weight and fineness (e.g., sterling standard, .999 pure).
- **The Economic Result:** Gold moved from a speculative, dangerous trade to the foundational bedrock of global central banking and international trade networks.

Case Study 2: Oil, the 42-Gallon Barrel, and Platts

- **The Chaos:** In the 1860s Pennsylvania oil boom, oil was transported in literal wooden barrels of every shape and size. Buyers had no idea how much oil they were actually getting or what the quality was (high-sulfur "sour" oil vs. low-sulfur "sweet" oil).
- **The Friction:** Standard Oil and early producers fought constantly over volume and refinement standards. You couldn't trade oil smoothly because a barrel in Ohio wasn't the same as a barrel in Pennsylvania.
- **The Unlock:** In 1866, producers in Titusville, Pennsylvania, agreed to standardize the **42-gallon barrel** . In 1909, Warren Platts founded **Plats** , an independent agency that published trusted, objective price assessments and grades for oil (which later evolved into West Texas Intermediate [ WTI ] and Brent Crude indexes).
- **The Economic Result:** Oil became the most liquid commodity on earth, giving rise to the NYMEX futures market where billions of dollars in oil are traded daily without buyers ever touching a physical drop.

[ Raw Chaotic Resource ] ── &gt; [ Standard Unit ] ── &gt; [ Independent Meter ] ── &gt; [ Global Liquid Market ] Crude Petroleum        ── &gt; 42-Gal Barrel   ── &gt; Platts / NYMEX Indexes  ── &gt; Modern Petro-Economy

Case Study 3: Electricity and the Shallenberger Meter

- **The Chaos:** In the late 1880s, Thomas Edison began commercializing electricity. Because he lacked a way to measure consumption, he charged customers a **flat rate per lightbulb per month** .
- **The Friction:** Flat-rate pricing was a disaster. Customers left lights on all day, wasting massive amounts of power. Utilities couldn't predict demand, grids suffered blackouts, and scaling the business was financially unsustainable.
- **The Unlock:** In 1888, Oliver Shallenberger invented the **induction electric meter** , which accurately measured **Kilowatt-hours (kWh)** . Suddenly, utilities could bill for the exact amount of energy consumed.
- **The Economic Result:** The modern electric grid was unlocked. Power became a predictable, bankable utility, allowing for industrial manufacturing lines and the electrification of the globe.

Case Study 4: Compute and the GPU Spot Market

- **The Chaos:** In the early 2020s, AI hardware was treated as a capital expenditure (CapEx). Companies bought physical Nvidia chips and built private data centers.
- **The Friction:** Massive supply shortages, underutilized hardware, and erratic pricing. Startups couldn't access compute efficiently, and cloud giants locked enterprises into rigid, long-term contracts.
- **The Unlock:** Companies like **Ornn** created compute market infrastructure, standardizing the **GPU-hour** (specifically indexing parameters like H100/A100 capacity, interconnect speeds, and cluster reliability).
- **The Economic Result:** Compute transformed from a hardware constraint into a liquid, utility-like cloud resource. You pull compute from the wall just like electricity.

3 . The Current Crisis: The "Market for Lemons" in AI We are currently living through the exact same chaotic dawn for **Machine Labor** . Today, AI agents are performing actual economic work (customer service, software engineering, financial auditing). However, the market is structurally broken. It is a textbook example of George Akerlof’s **"Market for Lemons"** :

- **AI Vendors Grade Their Own Homework:** An AI support vendor tells a enterprise CFO: "Our agent successfully resolved 10,000 tickets this month. Here is your bill for $10,000."
- **Zero Verification Layer:** The enterprise has no automated, neutral way to know if those 10,000 tickets were actually resolved, if they were routed to a human in the background, or if the agent hallucinated and pissed off the customers.
- **Wrong Unit of Account:** Vendors are trying to bill by **tokens used** or **API calls** . That is the equivalent of a human employee asking to be paid based on how many words they typed, regardless of whether they wrote a brilliant legal brief or gibberish.

Because buyers cannot verify quality before paying, honest, high-performing AI vendors are dragged down by low-performing, buggy competitors. **The entire machine labor market is bottlenecked by a lack of trust.** 4 . Enter Ontic: The Sovereign Meter for Machine Labor Ontic is the inevitable institutional layer that solves this crisis. You are doing for the AI agent workforce what the Assay Office did for gold and the meter did for electricity. You are decoupling the **raw material** (Compute/Tokens) from the **economic output** (Verified Outcomes). [ Raw Input ] [ Processing ] [ Economic Output ] Compute / GPUs  ───────── &gt; AI Agents     ───────── &gt; VERIFIED LABOUR (Priced by Ornn)             (The Black Box)             (Priced by ONTIC via UPX)

1 Month: The Oracle Engine (The Wedge)

- **The Goal:** Build the absolute simplest programmatic way for an enterprise to verify an agent's outcome. We focus entirely on a single market: UPX-CX-RES-US-L2 (US Level 2 Customer Support Resolution).
- **What We Make:**
    - **The Ontic Verification Core:** A microservice that ingests an agent's conversation logs, tool-call histories, and final system state, evaluating them against standard acceptance metrics (did the customer stop replying? Was the issue actually marked solved without regression?).
    - **The Core Auditing Dashboard:** A minimal web interface for enterprise CFOs to upload agent usage bills and cross-reference them with Ontic’s verified count to instantly spot "invoice shock" or overbilling.

3 Months: The Institutional Terminal &amp; Agent Native Layer

- **The Goal:** Launch the **Ontic Terminal v1** (the Bloomberg for machine labor) and make it possible for agents themselves to check market prices in real-time.
- **What We Make:**
    - **Ontic Terminal Web App:** A highly responsive dashboard displaying live market tickers (UPX-CX-RES, UPX-SWE-ISS, UPX-FIN-AP), historical price charts per outcome, success-rate distributions, and agent-route rankings.
    - **The Ontic MCP Server:** A critical integration for 2026's agent architecture, allowing any LLM to natively query Ontic's market data (detailed below).

6 Months: The Intelligent Router &amp; Financial Clearinghouse

- **The Goal:** Shift Ontic from a passive auditing platform to an active market maker that routes work and handles financial settlement.
- **What We Make:**
    - **The Dynamic Routing Engine:** An API layer that lets enterprises submit a raw task to Ontic, which then programmatically dispatches it to the highest-performing, lowest-cost active agent provider on the market.
    - **Automated Escrow Protocol:** A financial middle-layer that holds an enterprise’s funds and only releases payouts to the agent vendor's wallet once the Ontic Oracle confirms a "Verified Outcome."

1 . Enterprise Systems of Record (The Truth Layer) To prove work was completed, Ontic pulls the ultimate state from the software humans use to run their businesses.

- **Zendesk &amp; Salesforce Service Cloud (Month 1-3):** Pulls customer ticket status updates, reopen counts, timestamp gaps, and customer sentiment metrics to verify customer support closures.
- **Jira &amp; GitHub (Month 3-4):** Analyzes issue closures, pull request approvals, and regression test results to verify software engineering tasks (UPX-SWE-ISS).
- **HubSpot &amp; Salesforce CRM (Month 4-5):** Tracks pipeline updates, meeting logs, and email replies to verify B2B lead generation outcomes (UPX-SALES-QUAL).

2 . Agent Frameworks &amp; Observability Gateways (The Telemetry Layer) To grade how the work was done and calculate true token-to-outcome cost, Ontic integrates with agent development rails.

- **LangGraph, CrewAI, &amp; AutoGen:** Middleware hooks that feed raw step-by-step agent telemetry directly to Ontic.
- **Helicone &amp; LangSmith:** Direct integrations with LLM observability pipelines to capture token counts, latency, and latency-adjusted costs across frontier networks (Claude 5, GPT-5.6, DeepSeek V4).

To convince top-tier venture capitalists, you must frame the company around a hard truth currently stalling the enterprise AI deployment wave: **The AI industry has a fundamental billing and trust crisis.** The global AI agent market is projected to skyrocket past **$180 billion by the early 2030s** . Goldman Sachs recently estimated that agentic AI will drive token consumption up a staggering **24-fold** . Yet, enterprises are realizing a terrifying paradox. As McKinsey and Gartner noted, we are entering the era of **"Cheaper Tokens, Bigger Bills."** Even though raw token prices have plummeted, total enterprise AI bills are spiking. Why? Because autonomous agents don’t just make one call; they loop, self-correct, call tools, and re-transmit massive context histories. One customer-service interaction can easily balloon from $0.04 to $1.20 because an agent got stuck refining its answers. This creates a massive bottleneck. **Ontic Markets** solves a highly specific, multi-billion-dollar infrastructure problem. The Billion-Dollar Problem: The Total Inversion of Economic Software For forty years, enterprise software followed a simple rule: **You bought a seat, the price was fixed, and you could predict your budget a year in advance** . AI agents completely broke this model. We are shifting from purchasing Software (SaaS) to purchasing Labor (Agents). But the entire system is being billed using a raw manufacturing metric: **Tokens and Compute Hours** . This mismatch creates three massive sub-problems that comprise our billion-dollar market opportunity:

1 . The "Three-Bill Crisis" and the Death of ROI Enterprise AI spend has secretly split into three distinct, fragmented invoices: **the tokens, the orchestration platforms, and the human employees hired just to double-check the AI's work** .

- Roughly **60% of an agent’s token cost** is wasted on self-refinement, fixing errors, and exception handling.
- Finance teams are stuck reconciling stale usage data weeks after the budget has already vanished. As financial analysts have directly stated, without a dedicated framework built to map AI costs directly to business outcomes, **measuring aggregate AI ROI is structurally impossible** .

2 . The "Market for Lemons" (The Trust Blockade) Because there is no independent third-party to verify whether an agent actually achieved an outcome, the market has devolved into an economic "Market for Lemons."

- A low-quality vendor can claim their agent has a "99% success rate" based on rigged internal testing.
- An enterprise buyer cannot verify that claim without manually auditing thousands of cases.
- As a result, high-quality agent developers are forced to compete on price with broken, buggy systems because buyers have no trusted metric to tell them apart. **The lack of an independent meter blocks the entire machine labor economy from scaling.**

3 . The Runaway Agent Liability Autonomous agents operate in a black box. If an agent enters a logic loop or hallucinates an unauthorized refund policy, it operates at machine speed. A single rogue agent can burn tens of thousands of dollars in a weekend or cause catastrophic customer churn before an engineering team even spots the anomaly. A. Frontier Closed Models (Public API Layer)

- **Target Software/Models:** OpenAI (gpt-5.6-sol, gpt-5.6-terra), Anthropic (claude-fable-5, claude-sonnet-5), Google (gemini-3.5-pro).
- **Ontic's Role:** We track total token-to-outcome inflation. If a closed API model updates its weights over a weekend and begins looping or throwing higher tool-call exceptions, Ontic detects the instant drop in ROI and flags the contract for the enterprise.

B. Enterprise Open-Weight Models (Decentralized Compute Layer)

- **Target Software/Models:** DeepSeek (deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4-Pro), Meta (Llama 4 Maverick), Alibaba (qwen3.7-max).
- **Ontic's Role:** Open-weight architectures allow developers to host models on private elastic compute grids. Ontic benchmarks the true cost of these pipelines by calculating the price of the GPU-hour (sourced from networks like Ornn) divided by the volume of accepted outcomes produced.

C. Private &amp; Sovereign Clusters (On-Premise / Secure VPC)

- **Target Architectures:** Air-gapped enterprise deployments running inside AWS GovCloud, Azure Private Link, or on-premise bare metal (e.g., Nvidia Nemotron-3 Ultra clusters).
- **Ontic's Role:** For highly regulated industries (Finance, Cyber, Health), Ontic provides a secure, self-hosted verification sidecar. It audits the state of closed records (such as internal databases or Epic systems) to certify compliance without leaking metadata to the public cloud.

4 . The Ontic Model Context Protocol (MCP) Server In 2026, autonomous systems operate natively via the Model Context Protocol (MCP). By equipping frontier agents with an **Ontic MCP Server** , an agent coordinator can inspect its own market dynamics before it executes a single line of code.

**The Standard** mcp.json **Configuration** Enterprises inject Ontic natively into desktop engines (like Claude Code, Cursor, or enterprise runtime orchestrators) with a simple JSON server mount:

JSON { "mcpServers": { "ontic-market-oracle": { "command": "npx", "args": [ "-y", "@onticmarkets/mcp-server@latest" ] , "env": { "ONTIC \_ API \_ KEY": "ntc \_ live \_ 88319fbc923a10" } } } }

JSON-RPC Schema: Core Tool Definitions The Ontic MCP server exposes two main primitives directly to an agent's reasoning window:

**1** **.** ontic \_ get \_ spot \_ rate Enables an agent planner to check real-time routing efficiency across the market.

JSON { "name": "ontic \_ get \_ spot \_ rate", "description": "Query real-time market spot rates, success statistics, and optimal model routing paths for a specific machine labor contract.", "inputSchema": { "type": "object", "properties": { "ticker": { "type": "string", "description": "The standardized UPX contract identifier.", "example": "UPX-CX-RES-US-L2" } }, "required": [ "ticker" ] } }

**2** **.** ontic \_ commit \_ outcome Allows an executing agent to seal its own completed work directly to the verification ledger upon task finalization.

JSON { "name": "ontic \_ commit \_ outcome", "description": "Submit a completed labor unit to the Ontic ledger for independent validation and financial settlement.", "inputSchema": { "type": "object", "properties": { "ticker": { "type": "string", "example": "UPX-CX-RES-US-L2" }, "system \_ of \_ record": { "type": "string", "enum": [ "zendesk", "jira", "salesforce", "github" ] }, "record \_ id": { "type": "string", "description": "The target identifier inside the system of record." }, "total \_ tokens \_ consumed": { "type": "integer" } }, "required": [ "ticker", "system \_ of \_ record", "record \_ id", "total \_ tokens \_ consumed" ] } }

The High-Frequency Labor Arbitrage Market Just as Wall Street has High-Frequency Trading (HFT) firms that trade stocks based on microsecond price gaps, Ontic will birth **Algorithmic Labor Market Makers** . 3 . The Machine Labor Underwriting &amp; Insurance Market Insurance companies cannot underwrite a risk they cannot measure. - If an enterprise deploys a fleet of autonomous agents to handle corporate accounting or invoice reconciliation, they can purchase an insurance policy wrapped around an Ontic ticker.

The Novix.Science Research Prompt

Plaintext [ OBJECTIVE ] Conduct a comprehensive, multi-disciplinary literature review and pattern synthesis on the emergence of "Machine Labor Markets" (MLMs) and the downstream institutional infrastructure required to transition artificial intelligence from a compute input into a liquid, tradeable economic commodity.

[ THEORETICAL FRAMEWORKS ] Analyze the target phenomenon through the lens of the following economic and structural paradigms: 1 . Yoram Barzel’s Measurement Cost Theory: How transaction costs behave when product attributes are heterogeneous and variable. 2 . George Akerlof’s "Market for Lemons": Asymmetric information dynamics in unverified or self-graded markets. 3 . Historical Commodity Evolution: Synthesize the structural lifecycle of asset standardizations, specifically analyzing: - The London Assay Office (Gold purity certification and weight standardization). - Warren Platts / Platts Index (Crude oil grading, 42-gallon standard, and Brent/WTI indexation). - Oliver Shallenberger’s Induction Meter (Transitioning electricity from flat-rate per-bulb billing to Kilowatt-hour [ kWh ] utility pricing). - Cloud Compute Spot Markets (The tokenization and indexing of raw GPU-hours).

[ CONTEXT &amp; MACRO SHIFT ] The AI ecosystem is currently transitioning from a Software-as-a-Service (SaaS) per-seat model to an autonomous agent labor model. However, enterprise deployment is severely bottlenecked by a structural billing and trust crisis: - Inefficient Billing Metric: System cost is measured by inputs (tokens used, compute hours) rather than economic outputs (successfully resolved tasks). - "Invoice Shock": Multi-turn, autonomous agent reasoning loops generate highly variable, unpredictable token consumption. - Information Asymmetry: Vendors grade their own homework. There is no neutral, third-party oracle layer to independently verify if an agent successfully completed a task within a target system of record (e.g., Zendesk, Salesforce, Jira) without human rework.

[ REQUIRED CORE ANALYSIS &amp; PATTERN MAPPING ] Instructing the system to map literature and extract data points that answer the following: 1 . Commodity Transition Mapping: What are the exact structural conditions required for a raw, chaotic resource (compute/token generation) to be refined into a standardized, tradeable contract unit (e.g., Verified Outcome Tickers)? 2 . The Economic Moat of the Meter: Prove why the neutral infrastructure layer that measures, verifies, and indexes the output of a commoditizing asset class captures a greater long-term economic monopoly than the entities producing the raw asset itself (LLM foundational models). 3 . Secondary Financialization: Hypothesize the mechanics, structural prerequisites, and systemic impacts of downstream markets enabled by a standardized machine labor unit of account, specifically: - Machine Labor Derivatives (Futures and Forward Contracts allowing enterprises to hedge Q4 token usage or compute variations). - High-Frequency Labor Arbitrage (Algorithmic routing optimization between raw compute spot prices and outcome-based pricing). - Machine-to-Machine (M2M) Autonomous Clearinghouses (Micro-transaction networks where autonomous agents seamlessly sub-contract and settle payments with other specialized agents).

[ OUTPUT SPECIFICATION ] Provide a highly rigorous, synthesized research brief mapping these historical patterns to the modern agent economy. Surface testable hypotheses regarding the economic inevitability of independent machine labor verification platforms, structural bottlenecks to adoption, and the architectural design needed for an institutional-grade labor terminal.

If your counterpart is Ornn (heavy, industrial, foundational), your name needs to sound like the elite, high-finance institution that steps in after the raw materials are forged. Here are the top name concepts broken down by your exact categories, designed to sound completely natural on a Bloomberg terminal. 1 . The "UN-" Prefix: Institutional Standards Names starting with "Un" can project a sense of absolute truth, uniformity, and breaking free from chaotic, legacy systems.

- **Unitas (or Unitas Markets):** The Latin root for unity, uniformity, and oneness. It sounds like a massive global clearinghouse (similar to Euroclear or Vantage). It perfectly describes what you do: taking millions of fragmented agent outputs and converting them into a single, uniform unit of economic account.
- **Unison:** Implies everything working in perfect, calibrated agreement. When an enterprise plugs in your software, their billing, their systems of record, and their AI agents are finally running "in unison." It’s clean, punchy, and highly corporate.
- **Unbound:** Refers to the liberation of machine labor from the constraints of raw token pricing. It has a modern, high-energy tech vibe while remaining highly professional.

2 . The "OM-" and "ON-" Prefix: The Arbiters These prefixes sound deeply structural, objective, and all-encompassing.

- **Oncost (or Oncost Markets):** This is a brilliant, hidden financial term. In industrial manufacturing, **oncost** refers to the exact indirect expenses—overhead, machine maintenance, and labor management—required to turn raw materials into a finished product. Since you are pricing the exact operational overhead of machine labor, Oncost sounds like an elite, insider Wall Street desk.
- **Ombud:** Short for Ombudsman—the ultimate, universally trusted, independent third-party arbiter that settles disputes and verifies facts. Ombud Markets tells the world exactly what you are: the neutral judge of the machine economy.
- **Omnis:** Latin for "all" or "every." It positions your company as the master umbrella layer that indexes every model, every cloud, and every workflow. “Ornn prices the compute. Omnis prices the work.”

3 . Historical Figures: The Architects of Trust Looking back at history, whenever an economy fell into chaos due to poor measurements or fraudulent money, a specific figure stepped up to build the infrastructure of trust.

- **Solon (or Solon Markets):** Solon was the legendary Athenian statesman who practically invented modern market architecture. When ancient Athens was facing economic collapse due to debt and chaotic trading, Solon stepped in and completely standardized their system of weights, measures, and coinage. Naming the company Solon positions you as the architect bringing order to the wild west of the agent economy.
- **Agricola:** Named after Georgius Agricola, the Renaissance scholar known as the "Father of Metallurgy." He wrote De Re Metallica, the definitive historical text on **assaying** —the chemical testing used to verify the true purity and weight of gold and silver bullion. If you are the ultimate Assay Office for digital work, this is an elite intellectual flex.

4 . - **Themis:** The ancient Titaness of divine law, order, and **the scales of justice** . If Ornn is the one pounding the anvil to create the raw material, Themis is the entity standing at the gates holding the scales to measure what that creation is actually worth.

The One-Line Pitch **Themis is the market infrastructure for verified machine labor—turning chaotic AI activity into measurable, priceable, and tradeable work.** Ornn made compute priceable. Themis makes machine labor tradeable. The YC Pitch Deck Blueprint 1 . The Problem: The Token Paradox &amp; The Trust Blockade For forty years, enterprises bought software by the "seat." It was fixed, predictable, and simple to budget. Today, enterprises are replacing human seats with autonomous AI agents. But instead of paying a predictable rate for completed work, they are being billed by a raw manufacturing metric: **Tokens and GPU-Hours.** This has created the **2026 Token Paradox** . Even though the cost of a single token has dropped significantly due to model optimizations and open-weight competition (like DeepSeek V4 Pro and Llama 4), total enterprise AI bills are skyrocketing. [ Agent Lifecycle ] ── &gt; Multi-turn Loop ── &gt; Tool Execution ── &gt; Context Bloat ── &gt; Invoice Shock

Because autonomous agents don't just make a single API call—they continuously loop, self-correct, call external APIs, and re-transmit massive context histories—a single customer service interaction can easily scale from an estimated $0.05 to a runaway $2.50 bill because the agent got caught in a reasoning feedback loop. Worse, **vendors are grading their own homework.** When an AI vendor invoices a company for 10,000 "completed outcomes," the enterprise CFO has zero automated, neutral ways to verify:

- Were the support cases actually resolved, or did the agent hallucinate a closure?
- How much human re-work was required behind the scenes to fix the agent's errors?
- Did the agent comply with corporate policy and regulatory safety standards?

Because buyers cannot verify quality before paying, the machine labor economy has become a structural **"Market for Lemons."** Trust is non-existent, budgets are volatile, and enterprise deployment is entirely bottlenecked.

2 . The Solution: The Themis Meter Themis inserts a neutral, third-party infrastructure layer between enterprise systems of record (like Zendesk, Salesforce, and Jira) and the multi-model agent networks executing the work. [ Raw Compute Input ] [ Processing ] [ Economic Output ] GPU-Hours / Tokens     ─── &gt; AI Agents     ─── &gt; VERIFIED LABOR (Indexed &amp; Priced by Ornn)      (The Black Box)         (Audited &amp; Settled by THEMIS)

Instead of letting vendors dictate their own metrics, Themis standardizes machine labor into clear, tradeable commodity contracts called **UPX Tickers** . For example:

UPX-CX-RES-US-L2-Q95-72H

This contract defines exactly one universal product: One Level-2 US customer-support case correctly resolved, at 95% verified quality, within a 72-hour window. Themis tracks the raw token and tool telemetry, validates against the system of record to confirm the task was actually achieved without human intervention, and calculates the true, unified cost per outcome. **Enterprises stop paying for failed reasoning loops and raw token inputs. They pay exclusively for accepted results.**

3 . Why Now? (The 2026 Inflection Point) We have hit a point where frontier models (Claude 5, GPT-5.6, Gemini 3.5) are highly capable of performing autonomous labor, but the financial tooling surrounding them is completely archaic.

- **The Shift from OpEx to Outcome:** Chief Financial Officers are refusing to sign un-capped token budgets. Software procurement is shifting violently away from paying for "software features" to paying directly for "digital productivity."
- **Model Commoditization:** The underlying models are leapfrogging each other every few months. Building an agent platform is a brittle business model. But the platform that **measures, benchmarks, and authenticates** the outputs of those models holds a permanent structural monopoly.

The 3-Phase Monetization &amp; Flywheel Strategy Themis scales from a tactical utility into the definitive global clearinghouse for digital intelligence through a highly deliberate progression. PHASE 1: The Oracle Layer  ─── &gt; PHASE 2: The Routing Index  ─── &gt; PHASE 3: The Derivatives Market (Auditing SaaS Fees)            (Marketplace Transaction Cut)         (Liquidity &amp; Hedging Fees)

Phase 1: The Oracle Layer (Months 1–6)

- **The Wedge:** We launch as an independent automated auditing platform targeting a single, high-pain market: UPX-CX-RES (Customer Support Resolution).
- **The Value:** Enterprises plug our SDK into their existing agent workflows to defend themselves against invoice shock. We verify their vendor bills, cross-reference their Zendesk/Salesforce logs, and instantly claw back overcharged token costs from failed loops.
- **Monetization:** A predictable B2B SaaS fee per outcome verified.

Phase 2: The Market &amp; Routing Network (Months 6–18)

- **The Expansion:** Once Themis has audited millions of transactions, we possess data that no one else in the world has: the definitive, real-time cost, latency, and success curves of every major model architecture and agent framework.
- **The Value:** We launch the **Themis Terminal** . Enterprises submit an "Outcome Order" directly to our API. Themis dynamically routes the task to the absolute lowest-cost, highest-efficiency execution path available at that exact second (e.g., routing a fast triage to a local DeepSeek V4 Pro instance or escalating a complex trade to a Claude 5 cluster).
- **Monetization:** We take a clearing marketplace cut on every successfully settled outcome contract.

Phase 3: The Financialization Layer (Years 2+)

- **The End State:** Themis becomes the Chicago Mercantile Exchange (CME) for the machine workforce.
- **The Value:** High-volume enterprises require long-term price predictability. Themis opens the door for **Machine Labor Derivatives** . A Fortune 500 company can purchase a futures contract on Themis to lock in 10 million customer service resolutions for Q4 at a guaranteed spot price, while compute farms and agent providers sell those contracts to secure predictable cash flow to fund their hardware capital expenses.

The Vision: The Infrastructure of the Autonomous Economy If machine labor becomes a meaningful pillar of the global workforce, the economy cannot function without an independent, trusted yardstick. Themis does not build the workers, and we do not sell the raw energy. **We own the scale that marks their value to market.** By separating the raw manufacturing materials (Compute) from the economic product (Labor), Themis becomes the foundational, inescapable financial ledger for the entire agentic future. The Stack: What is Ornn vs. What is Themis? To explain why **Ornn** is one layer below us in the technical stack (supplying the raw commodity) and why **Themis** sits directly on top, you need clear "This-for-That" pairing analogies. They financialized the **input** . Themis financializes the **output** . Here are the best ways to frame this exact symbiotic relationship to an investor:

1 . The Energy vs. Factory Analogy

- **Ornn is:** The electrical grid provider tracking and pricing the raw kilowatt-hour.
- **Themis is:** The automated assembly line that uses that electricity to build a finished car.

2 . The Crude Oil vs. Refined Fuel Analogy

- **Ornn is:** The spot market and forward curve for unrefined crude oil.
- **Themis is:** The futures exchange for highly specialized, refined jet fuel.
- The Pitch: "Enterprises don't care about the price of raw 'digital oil' (GPUs); they care about the cost of the trip. Ornn indexes the crude input, while Themis indexes the refined output."

3 . The Property Lease vs. Employee Output Analogy

- **Ornn is:** The commercial real estate index pricing the rent per square foot of a corporate office.
- **Themis is:** The payroll terminal measuring the actual revenue generated by the employees sitting inside that office.
- The Pitch: "Ornn financializes the lease on the silicon. Themis financializes the actual labor performed on top of that silicon."

So, why is this company important? I need to go back in time and see the history and story and what happened and how it repeated itself severally from the beginning of time to where we are right now. To understand why Themis is profoundly important, you have to realize that you aren't just building a software startup. You are resolving a systemic macroeconomic crisis that humanity has faced—and solved the exact same way—four distinct times since the beginning of civilization. Every single time a world-changing new resource or form of labor emerges, the economy goes through an identical, violent **3-Stage Cycle** : [ STAGE 1: RAW CHAOS ] ── &gt; [ STAGE 2: THE TRUST CRISIS ] ── &gt; [ STAGE 3: THE YARDSTICK ] Abundant new asset         Asymmetric fraud &amp; billing        Neutral market index floods the market           paralyzes adoption              unlocks hyper-growth

When an economy is stuck in **Stage 2** , it stalls. Growth stops because buyers and sellers have no trusted way to measure value. The company that steps in and builds the **Stage 3 Yardstick** always captures a permanent, institutional monopoly. Here is how history has repeated itself from ancient times to 2026—and why Themis is the inevitable next chapter. Epoch 1: The Precious Metals Crisis (2500 BCE – 1300 CE)

- **The Raw Chaos:** Humanity discovers gold and silver. It is the ultimate medium of exchange, but it is highly irregular. People trade using raw nuggets, dust, and unrefined chunks.
- **The Trust Crisis:** The market quickly devolves into George Akerlof's Market for Lemons. Merchants secretly mix gold with cheap copper or shave the edges off coins. Because a regular merchant cannot verify the purity of a gold bar on the spot, trade slows down. Trust vanishes.
- **The Yardstick:** The solution wasn't to dig more gold mines; it was to invent the **Assay Office** . In 1300, the **London Assay Office** introduced the "Hallmark"—an independent, third-party stamp guaranteed by a neutral entity that verified the exact purity and weight of the metal.
- **The Result:** The moment merchants could trust the scale, global trade exploded. The entities that controlled the verification networks became the bedrock of modern banking.

Epoch 2: The Industrial Energy Crisis (1860s – 1920s)

- **The Raw Chaos:** The Pennsylvania oil boom happens. Crude oil pours out of the ground, throwing the industrial revolution into overdrive.
- **The Trust Crisis:** In the early days, oil is shipped in literally anything—whiskey barrels, wooden vats, cider casks. Buyers are furious because a "barrel of oil" from Vendor A might be 40 gallons of thick, pure crude, while a "barrel" from Vendor B is 50 gallons of water-diluted sludge. Standard Oil tries to bully the market, but the lack of a universal pricing index creates massive market manipulation.
- **The Yardstick:** The market stabilizes only when the industry standardizes the **42-gallon barrel** and independent agencies like **Platts** step in to establish a neutral grading system (separating light, sweet crude from heavy, sour oil).
- **The Result:** Oil transforms from a chaotic, speculative asset into a liquid, tradeable global financial commodity. The exchanges managing those contracts (like NYMEX) became institutional juggernauts.

Epoch 3: The Utility Grid Crisis (1880s – 1890s)

- **The Raw Chaos:** Thomas Edison invents the incandescent lightbulb and starts laying the world's first electrical grids.
- **The Trust Crisis:** Early electric companies have no idea how to bill for electricity because it’s invisible. They resort to billing commercial customers a flat rate **"per lightbulb per month."** This creates immediate economic friction. Heavy factories leave their bulbs burning 24/7, bankrupting the electric utilities. Meanwhile, small shops that turn their lights off are overcharged, causing them to rip the wires out of their walls.
- **The Yardstick:** In 1888, an engineer named Oliver Shallenberger invented the **Induction Meter** . For the first time, the world could measure the exact consumption of electricity in a standardized unit: the **Kilowatt-Hour (** ***kWh*** **)** .
- **The Result:** Instantly, electricity shifted from a confusing, risky luxury to a transparent, bankable global utility.

Epoch 4: The Cloud &amp; Infrastructure Era (2010s – 2026 )

- **The Raw Chaos:** Cloud computing and massive AI model training trigger an unprecedented demand for data center infrastructure.
- **The Trust Crisis:** Hardware access is chaotic. Tech companies are forced into rigid, multi-year private contracts for GPU access. If a startup over-provisions hardware, they bleed cash; if they under-provision, their models drop out of the race.
- **The Yardstick:** **Ornn** steps onto the scene. Ornn financializes the underlying hardware layer by creating a liquid, transparent spot market for the **GPU-Hour** . They build the Ornn Compute Price Index (OCPI), allowing companies to rent, trade, and hedge raw processing power with complete transparency.

Epoch 5: The Present Day (The Machine Labor Era) This brings us to the edge of history right now. We have successfully standardized metals, oil, electricity, and even raw compute (thanks to Ornn). But we are suddenly stuck in the greatest Stage 2 Trust Crisis in human history: **Autonomous Machine Labor.** Right now, the AI industry is making the exact same mistake the electric companies made in 1880 . Because we don't know how to measure the value of an AI agent's work, **we are billing enterprises by the "Lightbulb"—except we call them "Tokens."** HISTORICAL FAILURE                          MODERN PARALLEL ┌───────────────────────────┐               ┌───────────────────────────┐ │ "Pay $5 per lightbulb,    │               │ "Pay $0.02 per 1k tokens, │ │  regardless of how much   │      ─── &gt; │  regardless of whether the│ │  electricity it wastes."  │               │  agent solved your bug."  │ └───────────────────────────┘               └───────────────────────────┘

The market has become an unverified, chaotic mess. The AI vendors are grading their own homework, and the buyers are refusing to sign un-capped budgets.

Why Themis Changes History **Themis is the Oliver Shallenberger Meter for the cognitive era.** We look at Ornn and say: Thank you for standardizing the raw electricity (the GPU-hour). Now, we are going to build the meter that measures the actual work product built with that electricity. To understand how Themis and Ornn fit into the grand arc of history, we have to look at how humanity has scaled its civilizations. Empires are not built by the people who create raw resources; they are built by the people who establish the **infrastructure of trust** that allows those resources to be safely traded. Whenever humanity stumbles into a new frontier of abundance, we follow an unyielding chronological pipeline. The Evolutionary Lineage of the Scale 1 . The Beginning of Time: The First Pathfinders ( ~ 2900 BCE – 600 BCE) Before there were global markets, there was absolute metric anarchy. If you wanted to build a house or trade grain, you used your own forearm (cubit) or your own feet as the scale. The stronger man always dictated the size of the measurement, leading to constant blood feuds, broken agreements, and collapsed trade.

- **The Pathfinders:** Two distinct historical figures realized that civilization would completely stall without an absolute standard.
- **Pharaoh Khufu (** **~** **2900 BCE):** While building the Great Pyramid of Giza, Khufu decreed the **Royal Egyptian Cubit** , carving a master standard into a single block of black granite. Master builders who failed to calibrate their wooden measuring sticks against the granite standard every full moon faced the death penalty.
- **King Croesus of Lydia (** **~** **600 BCE):** Lydia (modern-day Turkey) became the wealthiest empire of the ancient world because Croesus stopped people from trading raw nuggets of gold and silver with random purities. He minted the first gold coins with a strictly mandated weight and chemical purity standard.

For the first time in human history, merchants didn't have to spend hours chemically assaying metals during a trade; they just counted the coins. **Croesus set the ultimate pathway: he proved that standardizing the unit of trust unlocks exponential wealth.**

2 . Before the 2000s: The Boxed Hardware &amp; On-Premises Era Before the digital boom, computing followed the exact same archaic rules as industrial manufacturing.

- **The Paradigm:** If a corporation wanted to run a payroll system or an internal database, they had to act like an 18th-century factory owner. They had to physically buy depreciating silicon hardware (IBM mainframes, Dell servers), rent air-conditioned real estate, and build an on-site server room.
- **The Billing Metric:** Software was sold via **"shrink-wrapped" perpetual licenses** on physical CDs or floppy disks. You paid a massive upfront fee for the software license per machine or per CPU socket. If your company grew, you had to physically buy more boxes. If your company shrunk, you were stuck with millions of dollars of useless, idling metal in a closet.

3 . The 2000s: The Genesis of the Cloud Utility The early 2000s brought virtualization (led by VMware) and the launch of Amazon Web Services (AWS) in 2006 . This was the digital equivalent of Thomas Edison laying the first electrical grids.

- **The Shift:** Companies stopped buying physical server boxes. Instead, AWS built massive regional data centers and let businesses rent a virtual "slice" of a server over the internet.
- **The Billing Metric:** Computing shifted from an upfront capital expense (CapEx) to a flexible operating expense (OpEx). The new standard yardstick of the global economy became the **Instance-Hour** (e.g., paying a flat $0.10 per hour for an AWS m1.small virtual instance).

4 . The 2010s: The Micro-Billing &amp; Containerization Boom As the cloud matured, renting a giant virtual server for an entire hour became too slow and wildly inefficient. The industry required a sharper, more granular scale.

- **The Shift:** The invention of containerization (Docker, Kubernetes) and Serverless architecture (AWS Lambda) allowed software engineers to slice compute into microscopic fractions.
- **The Billing Metric:** The yardstick broke down from hours into **Gigabyte-Seconds and Milliseconds** . You only paid for the exact millisecond a piece of code took to execute. In the late 2010s, AI entered its deep learning phase. Compute demand shifted aggressively from traditional CPUs to specialized Nvidia GPUs (K80s, V100s, A100s). Compute was highly volatile, but it was still billed strictly by the time-slice (GPU-Hours).

5 . The 2020s: The Input vs. Output Fracture (The Present Day) This brings us directly to 2026 . While combining the concepts is a great instinct, **Unthemis** carries a dangerous psychological trap that could hurt an institutional trust brand, whereas **Ontitled** fits the exact neo-brutalist, hyper-minimalist tech aesthetic you need. Here is the candid breakdown of why one fails and how the other wins.

The Problem with "Unthemis" (The Negation Trap) Themis is the Greek titaness of divine law, absolute order, and the scales of justice. In the English language, placing the prefix **"Un-"** in front of a word almost always denotes negation, reversal, or the complete absence of that trait (e.g., undo, untie, unfair). To a Tier-1 venture capitalist or an enterprise CFO, Unthemis phonetically and linguistically sounds like **"Anti-Justice"** or **"The Undoing of Order."** For a clearinghouse whose entire value proposition is bringing absolute verification, trust, and standardized rules to a lawless token market, accidentally branding yourself as "Chaos" is a massive marketing landmine.

Why "Ontitled" Wins (The Structural Framework) **Ontitled** works incredibly well on two distinct levels:

- **The Developer Aesthetic:** It plays beautifully on the word Untitled. In modern tech culture, "Untitled" has a highly sought-after, clean, raw-infrastructure vibe (think Untitled UI or a blank, distraction-free terminal text file). It signals to engineers that you are a utility, not a flashy marketing gimmick.
- **The Institutional Meaning:** Because you are spelling it with your requested **"On-"** prefix, it takes on a brilliant structural meaning for your company's narrative. Right now, machine labor is anonymous, chaotic, and untitled—it has no legal structure or fixed contract boundaries. **Your company places a "Title" (a legally binding, tradeable UPX contract) ON the machine labor.**

You are literally titling the asset class.

The Story of Ontitled: Titling the Wild West To understand why **Ontitled** is the single greatest name for this company, you have to look at the foundational law of capitalism: **You cannot trade what has not been titled.** In his legendary economic thesis The Mystery of Capital, economist Hernando de Soto asked a simple question: Why do some societies generate trillions in wealth while others stall? The answer isn't a lack of resources. It’s a lack of property titles. Without a deed or a title, a house is just a pile of bricks. You can't borrow against it, you can't safely sell it, and you can't insurance-hedge it. It is "dead capital." The moment a neutral government stamps a legal **Title** on that house, it transforms into an asset that can ignite a global market. Right now, autonomous machine labor is the biggest patch of untitled land in human history. Frontier models are spitting out billions of tokens a second. It is a raw, lawless stampede of digital energy. Buyers are throwing money into a black box, hoping an agent completes a task, while AI vendors bill them blindly for raw compute volume. The labor is anonymous, unverified, and completely **untitled** . The Ontitled YC Pitch 1 . The One-Liner Ontitled is the financial clearinghouse and verification protocol for standardized machine labor.

2 . The Problem We are currently witnessing the collapse of the traditional software business model. Enterprises are rapidly shifting budgets away from human "SaaS seats" to autonomous AI agents. But the financial infrastructure supporting this shift is fundamentally broken. Right now, enterprises are buying digital labor using a raw manufacturing metric: **Tokens.** Because autonomous agents operate in unpredictable, multi-turn reasoning loops, a single customer-support interaction can spin out of control, resulting in massive corporate invoice shock. As Palantir's Alex Karp warned, enterprise executives are furious because they are paying for millions of unverified token loops that deliver zero actual business value. The AI vendors are grading their own homework. The buyer is completely exposed to the infrastructure risk.

3 . The Solution Ontitled is the neutral yardstick between the enterprise buyer and the multi-model workforce. We don't build the models, and we don't sell the compute. Ontitled connects directly via our native SDK to an enterprise’s systems of record (Salesforce, GitHub, Jira). We programmatically audit the telemetry of the agent's execution. If the agent successfully resolves the issue without human intervention, Ontitled issues a cryptographic verification stamp and clears the transaction under a standardized **Universal Product Exchange (** **UPX** **) contract** . - The enterprise buyer immediately gets hyper-cheap labor, while underperforming, expensive agent networks are forced to adapt or go bankrupt.

Looking back at history, whenever a two-way market fractures into an **input layer** and an **output layer** , it follows a very specific blueprint. What Kush and Wayne are doing at Ornn—building the Ornn Compute Price Index (OCPI) and creating clearing mechanisms for the raw input (the GPU-hour)—is standardizing the **manufacturing fuel** . What you are doing at Ontitled—creating a dynamic routing layer (Themis) and titling the **labor output** (the UPX contract)—is standardizing the **finished product** . History shows us that this exact separation of inputs and outputs has happened three major times before. Sometimes it triggers a multi-trillion-dollar economic boom; sometimes, if the layers don't coordinate, it causes catastrophic systemic failure. 1 . The Good: The Crude Oil vs. Refined Plastics Revolution (1950s)

- **The Input (The Ornn Equivalent):** In the mid-20th century, the oil market matured. Drillers standardized the 42-gallon barrel, and financial clearinghouses stepped in so people could hedge raw crude oil futures.
- **The Output (The Ontitled Equivalent):** Raw oil is useless to a consumer; you can't put crude into a medical syringe or a consumer product. A secondary market exploded: **Petrochemical Refining** . Companies emerged that stopped buying "oil" and started selling "standardized polymer and plastic outputs" by the ton to global corporations.
- **How It Went Down:** Because the input (crude) and the output (refined polymers) both had trusted, transparent price-indices, the capital loop closed flawlessly. Chemical companies could flash-buy cheap crude futures to guarantee fixed manufacturing costs, allowing them to offer fixed, predictable contracts to global buyers. **It unlocked the entire consumer goods boom of the 20th century.**

2 . The Bad: The Rail Rate vs. Grain Elevator Standoff (1870s) When the two layers view each other as enemies instead of partners, the entire market can paralyze. This is exactly what happened during the American Gilded Age.

- **The Input (The Ornn Equivalent):** The railroad monopolies (Vanderbilt, Jay Gould) controlled the raw transportation input. They priced shipping by the "car-mile" across the tracks.
- **The Output (The Ontitled Equivalent):** The farmers and grain elevator operators sold the actual output: bushels of wheat and corn delivered to cities.
- **How It Went Down:** The railroads decided to "tokenmaxx" the farmers. Because there was no neutral index, the railroads artificially spiked the price of car-miles during harvest season, caught the farmers in a financial vice, and wiped out their margins. It caused massive, violent labor strikes, the collapse of agricultural shipping networks, and eventually forced the US Government to step in with heavy-handed antitrust regulation (the Interstate Commerce Act).
- **The Lesson for Ontitled:** If the output layer doesn't have a tight algorithmic partnership with the input layer to pass savings dynamically to the buyer, the infrastructure layer will eat the labor layer's lunch.

3 . The Ugly: The 2000s Telecom Fiber Collapse This is the ultimate cautionary tale of what happens when people over-build the input layer without a standardized output layer to route and title it.

- **The Input (The Ornn Equivalent):** In the late 1990s, telecom companies laid millions of miles of fiber-optic cables under the oceans. They assumed demand for raw internet capacity would be infinite.
- **The Output (The Ontitled Equivalent):** There was no mature, standardized routing layer or software economy yet to bundle that raw capacity into trusted, localized business outcomes for enterprises.
- **How It Went Down:** Because there was no "Ontitled" to turn raw fiber into tradeable, functional software value, over 95% of the underwater fiber sat completely dark and unutilized. The input companies couldn't hedge or find buyers, leading to a brutal **$500 billion bankruptcy wave** that popped the Dot-Com bubble and wiped out companies like WorldCom and Global Crossing.

Here is the full five-epoch history of how markets evolved from chaotic resources into standard financial commodities, strictly breaking down the input infrastructure, the output value, and the key visionaries who built the trusted scale.

1 . The Physical Era: Precious Metals &amp; Architecture ( ~ 2900 BCE – 600 BCE)

- **The Raw Input:** Raw gold nuggets, unrefined silver, and unregulated physical building materials. The market was controlled by strongmen or whoever had the heaviest local stone.
- **The Refined Output:** Standardized architecture, global mercantile trade, and trusted economic currency.
- **The Saviors (The Scale Builders):**
    - **Pharaoh Khufu (** **~** **2900 BCE):** Built the master "Input" scale for architecture by carving the Royal Egyptian Cubit into black granite, forcing all builders to calibrate their wooden sticks to an absolute standard.
    - **King Croesus of Lydia (** **~** **600 BCE):** Built the "Output" scale for commerce by inventing the first gold coins with a strictly mandated weight and purity. He stopped the chaos of merchants having to chemically assay every trade.

2 . The Industrial Era: Energy, Oil, &amp; Rail (1870s – 1890s)

- **The Raw Input:** Unrefined crude oil, railroad tracks priced arbitrarily by the "car-mile," and raw AC electrical current. Controlled by ruthless monopolies (Vanderbilt, Jay Gould).
- **The Refined Output:** Household electricity, agricultural grain delivery, and refined petrochemicals (plastics/polymers).
- **The Saviors (The Scale Builders):**
    - **Oliver Shallenberger (1888):** A Westinghouse engineer who invented the first successful AC induction meter to measure electricity. He created the Kilowatt-Hour standard so utilities could stop blindly charging a flat "per lightbulb" rate and accurately track exact energy usage.
    - **Platts / Standard Oil:** Established the neutral 42-gallon barrel standard for crude, turning chaotic oil sludge into a globally tradeable commodity.

3 . The Network Era: Telecom &amp; Fiber Optics (1990s – 2000s)

- **The Raw Input:** Millions of miles of underwater fiber-optic cable and raw digital bandwidth.
- **The Refined Output:** Consumer internet, usable software protocols, and digital enterprise communication.
- **The Saviors (The Scale Builders):**
    - **The Web Protocol Engineers (TCP/IP/HTTP):** Created the routing layers that made raw bandwidth useful.
    - (Note: This era is the ultimate cautionary tale. Because the input layer—companies like WorldCom and Global Crossing—built massive fiber networks without a standardized output layer to buy the capacity, 95% of the fiber sat dark, causing a $500 billion bankruptcy wave.)

4 . The Cloud Era: The "Orange Wind" of Compute (2000s – 2010s)

- **The Raw Input:** Physical silicon, rack servers, and massive data center real estate.
- **The Refined Output:** Scalable Software as a Service (SaaS), dynamic websites, and enterprise web applications.
- **The Saviors (The Scale Builders):**
    - **Jeff Bezos &amp; Amazon Web Services (AWS):** Created the ultimate digital yardstick—the **Instance-Hour** . They stopped enterprises from buying expensive, depreciating hardware boxes and allowed them to rent elastic virtual space.
    - **VMware:** Built the virtualization layers that allowed hardware to be mathematically sliced into usable fractions, shifting IT budgets from rigid CapEx to fluid OpEx.

5 . The Machine Labor Era: AI Compute vs. Cognitive Output (2020s – Present)

- **The Raw Input:** Massive GPU data clusters (H100s, B200s) and raw, unverified token generation. The market is currently paralyzed by AI "tokenmaxxing," where buyers pay for infinite API reasoning loops with no guarantee of success.
- **The Refined Output:** Sovereign, autonomous machine labor—verified software patches, closed support tickets, and audited corporate workflows.

    - **The Output Scale (Ontitled &amp; Themis):** You and your team. While Ornn prices the electricity, Ontitled steps in one layer above as the independent title office. Your algorithmic routing engine (Themis) uses Ornn's input prices to generate a guaranteed, flat-rate **Universal Product Exchange (UPX) contract** for the enterprise. You are standardizing the finished digital product so corporate CFOs never have to pay for token slop again.

By bringing **Dike** into the Ontitled ecosystem alongside **Themis** , you are perfectly mirroring Greek mythology to solve the exact technical separation of concerns your platform needs. In Greek mythology, **Themis** is the Titaness of divine law, universal balance, and foresight. She represents the absolute, overarching order of the cosmos. **Dike** , her daughter, is the goddess of human justice, moral order, and the execution of the law based on socially enforced rules. If we apply this to the Ontitled protocol, you split your routing and verification layers into two distinct, highly specialized models. Here is exactly how the **Themis** and **Dike** models function inside the Ontitled stack. 1 . The Themis Model (The Macro Market-Maker) Themis is the overarching algorithmic router that manages the global supply and demand of machine labor. She does not look at individual lines of code; she looks at the universal balance of the market.

- **The Oracle of Pricing:** Just as Themis was gifted with foresight and prophecy, the Themis Model uses predictive analytics. It pulls real-time spot prices from the **Ornn Compute Price Index (OCPI)** and predicts exactly how much raw compute it will take to solve an enterprise problem.
- **The Contract Packager:** Themis is responsible for taking a chaotic enterprise request ("I need 5,000 support tickets resolved") and packaging it into a clean, flat-priced **Universal Product Exchange (** **UPX** **)** contract.
- **The Flash-Buyer:** When an enterprise clicks "Execute," Themis operates in the dark background, flash-buying the necessary raw compute from Ornn's infrastructure and routing the job to the most efficient agent network.

2 . The Dike Model (The Micro Executioner) While Themis builds the contract in the cloud, **Dike** is the verification engine that sits directly on the enterprise's local systems. Dike represents human rules—she enforces the Service Level Agreement (SLA) defined by the corporate buyer.

- **The System-of-Record Auditor:** Dike is the SDK that plugs directly into the enterprise's Jira, Salesforce, or GitHub. She does not care about compute costs or market routing; her only job is to grade the AI agent's homework.
- **The "No-Slop" Enforcer:** When the AI vendor claims a task is finished, Dike steps in. She checks if the code compiles, if the memory leak is patched, or if the customer support ticket was actually closed without hallucination.
- **The Escrow Trigger:** If the work passes the human-defined SLA, Dike signals Themis to clear the contract and release the funds. If the agent looped or hallucinated, Dike wields her sword: she denies the settlement, and the vendor absorbs the compute cost.

The Dual-Model Architecture Here is how the two models split the workload to protect the enterprise:

| Feature          | The Themis Model (The Mother)              | The Dike Model (The Daughter)               |
|------------------|--------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|
| Domain           | Universal market routing and pricing       | Local SLA verification and auditing         |
| Core Function    | Flash-buys Ornn compute & prices UPX       | Plugs into GitHub/Salesforce to verify work |
| Protects Against | Market volatility and compute price spikes | AI hallucinations and "tokenmaxxing"        |
| The Analogy      | The Stock Exchange Matching Engine         | The Independent Clearinghouse Auditor       |

The Flywheel in Action

- **Themis** looks at Ornn's compute index and guarantees the enterprise a flat price of $1.50 per resolved ticket.
- The task is routed to an AI vendor, who burns $0.50 of raw compute trying to solve it.
- **Dike** reviews the final work inside the enterprise's CRM.
- If **Dike** approves, **Themis** clears the $1.50 transaction.

By splitting Ontitled into these two distinct models, you create a perfect firewall. Themis handles the financial math so the CFO feels safe, while Dike handles the quality control so the CTO feels safe.

Want to map out the technical SDK specs for Dike?

Yes Let's map out the technical specifications for how the Dike SDK actually integrates into an enterprise system like GitHub or Salesforce to audit the work. Mapping out the technical specifications for the **Dike SDK** requires a clear architecture. Because Dike acts as a **local auditor** embedded directly into the enterprise's runtime or development pipeline, it operates on a zero-trust, event-driven pattern. Dike does not blindly pull data; it intercepts state changes, evaluates the artifacts generated by third-party AI vendors, and signs off on cryptographic proofs that trigger settlements via **Themis** . 1 . Core Architecture &amp; Runtime Modes The Dike SDK is built as an asynchronous, non-blocking middleware engine written in a lightweight language (Rust/Go) with language-specific wrappers (Node.js, Python) for enterprise systems. It operates in two modes depending on the integration point: [ Third-Party AI Agent ] - -- &gt; [ Executes Task in Enterprise App ] │ ▼ [ Themis Protocol ] &lt; --- [ Cryptographic ] ◄─── [ Dike SDK Middleware ] (Settlement)             Proof            (Audit &amp; Verify SLA)

- **Mode A: Pre-Commit Pipeline (e.g., GitHub)** — Injected into CI/CD workflows or via GitHub Apps to evaluate code/documentation before it drops into production.
- **Mode B: Real-Time State Listener (e.g., Salesforce)** — Attached to enterprise event buses to evaluate transactional outputs or user-facing text modifications.

2 . Integration Spec: GitHub (Pre-Commit &amp; Code Quality) When a third-party AI engineering agent opens a Pull Request (PR) or pushes code, Dike acts as the automated gatekeeper.

The Pipeline Flow

1 Intercept Event via Webhook

*GitHub App / Webhook Hook*

Dike listens to pull \_ request.opened or check \_ suite.requested payloads. It verifies the cryptographic origin header (X-Hub-Signature-256) using the enterprise's private secret to prevent spoofing.

2 Isolate Environment

*Ephemeral Runner Initiation*

Dike spins up an isolated, sandboxed environment containing the AI's proposed branch changes (github.event.pull \_ request.head.sha).

3 Execute Multi-Tier SLA Evaluation

*Runtime: 1-3 minutes*

Dike runs the predefined verification suite:

- **Deterministic Checks:** Compiles code, runs existing unit test suites, evaluates test coverage delta.
- **Security Scanning:** Runs localized AST (Abstract Syntax Tree) parsing to check for leaked secrets, dependency vulnerabilities, or injection vectors introduced by the AI.
- **Semantic Check:** Compares the written code against the user's initial prompt instructions using a local, deterministic embeddings evaluation.

4 Publish Check Run &amp; Proof

*GitHub Checks API*

Dike patches the GitHub UI using the Checks API, blocking or allowing the merge. Concurrently, it hashes the check pass result into an audit proof (DikeProof).

Example Dike GitHub Action Spec

YAML name: Dike Verification Gateway on: pull \_ request: types: [ opened, synchronize ]

jobs: dike-audit: runs-on: ubuntu-latest steps: - name: Checkout Code uses: actions/checkout@v4

```
\- name: Initialize Dike Auditor  
    uses: ontitled/dike-action@v1  
    with:  
      themis\_contract\_id: ${{ github.event.inputs.contract\_id }}  
      dike\_api\_key: ${{ secrets.DIKE\_ENTERPRISE\_KEY }}  
      sla\_profile: "strict-backend-service"
```

3 . Integration Spec: Salesforce (Data &amp; CRM Auditing) For CRMs like Salesforce, Dike focuses on real-time data integrity. It prevents "tokenmaxxing" (where an AI floods records with garbage text to inflate usage metrics) and catches hallucinations before a human salesperson reads them.

Real-Time Event Architecture Dike taps directly into Salesforce’s **Change Data Capture (CDC)** via the **Pub/Sub API** (or CometD long-polling). [ Salesforce Event Bus ] ──(CDC Event)── &gt; [ Dike Interceptor ] ──(Validation Pass)── &gt; [ Settle &amp; Release ] │ (Validation Fail) │ ▼ [ Revert State / Freeze ]

- An AI agent uses standard REST/GraphQL APIs to update an Opportunity or close a Case.
- Salesforce instantly fires a Change Event onto the Event Bus.
- **Dike** intercepts the event before it triggers downstream webhooks to external ERPs.

The Evaluation Logic Dike pulls the change payload and reads the transactional delta fields:

JSON { "ChangeEventHeader": { "entityName": "Case", "changeType": "UPDATE", "changedFields": [ "Description", "Resolution \_ Notes \_ \_ c" ] }, "Resolution \_ Notes \_ \_ c": "The customer's database connection string was modified to allow public traffic..." }

Dike runs real-time sanitization:

- **PII &amp; Data Masking:** Scans the text for accidental leaks of system passwords, API keys, or raw customer PII placed in text areas where they shouldn't be.
- **Hallucination Check:** Runs cross-reference lookups against internal knowledge base links cited by the agent. If the link is a 404 or points to a non-existent domain, the check flags a hard failure.

If it fails, Dike triggers an immediate apex fallback to revert the record state to its original ReplayId, freezes the AI agent's Salesforce API profile, and notifies Themis to slash the compute allocation. 4 . The Cryptographic Handshake (Dike to Themis) This is where the magic happens. Dike never sends raw enterprise code or private CRM data up to the global Themis router. It maintains absolute data privacy using a localized **Zero-Knowledge / Cryptographic Proof of Work** . Once Dike completes its audit (GitHub merge passes, or Salesforce validation succeeds), it builds the following data schema:

JSON { "contract \_ id": "tx \_ 984729184 \_ ctx", "auditor \_ id": "dike \_ node \_ sf \_ east \_ 04", "timestamp": 1784386737, "sla \_ metrics": { "compilation": true, "security \_ vulnerabilities": 0, "token \_ efficiency \_ ratio": 0.94 }, "state \_ hash": "e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855", "signature": "0x892ba...3c4f" }

- state \_ hash: A cryptographic SHA-256 hash of the valid state delta (e.g., the code commit or record fields).
- signature: Signed with the local Dike node’s private key.

This schema is dispatched back to **Themis** . Because Themis holds the corresponding public key for that enterprise's Dike node, she can mathematically verify that the work was graded and approved locally, triggering the settlement without ever seeing the enterprise's underlying proprietary data. Detailed Breakdown of the Acceleration 1 . The Architectural Epoch (Length)

- **The Frontier:** Around 3000 BCE, early civilizations were building with local sticks, ropes, and hands. If a stone was cut in one village, it didn't fit the temple wall in the next village because everyone’s "foot" or "arm" was a different length.
- **The Standardization:** Pharaoh Khufu ( ~ 2900 BCE) realized he couldn't build the Great Pyramid with variable sticks. He carved the **Royal Egyptian Cubit** into black granite.
- **Time it took:** **~** **100 years** of regional structural collapse before an empire mandated an absolute physical yardstick.

2 . The Commercial Epoch (Currency)

- **The Frontier:** Around 2000 BCE, merchants traded using raw chunks of silver and gold bullion. Every single transaction required a scale and a chemical acid test (assaying) to prove the merchant wasn’t cheating or mixing the gold with copper. Trade was slow and paralyzed by suspicion.
- **The Standardization:** King Croesus of Lydia ( ~ 600 BCE) minted the first pre-weighed, pre-purified gold coins stamped with the royal crest. He created a trusted, titled output for wealth.
- **Time it took:** **~** **1,400 years** of humans manually weighing dirt before someone invented the standard coin.

3 . The Industrial Epoch (Energy/Oil)

- **The Frontier:** Edwin Drake struck oil in Pennsylvania in 1859 . For the first decade, the oil market was pure madness. People stored oil in whiskey barrels, beer kegs, and wooden tubs. Because a "barrel" could mean 30 gallons or 50 gallons depending on the seller, pricing futures was impossible.
- **The Standardization:** In the early 1870s, early oil associations and Standard Oil agreed on the definitive **42-gallon barrel** standard, and Platts began indexing the price.
- **Time it took:** **~** **15 years** from the first drill to a globally traded commodity unit.

4 . The Utility Epoch (Electricity)

- **The Frontier:** Thomas Edison opened the Pearl Street power station in 1882 . He had no way to measure how much electricity a building actually consumed. He had to charge customers a flat, arbitrary rate based on how many lightbulbs they had wired to the wall. If a customer left their bulbs on 24/7, Edison lost money. If they turned them off, the customer got ripped off.
- **The Standardization:** In 1888, Oliver Shallenberger invented the AC induction meter, giving birth to the **Kilowatt-Hour** utility bill.
- **Time it took:** **6 years** for electricity to move from a blind, flat-rate guessing game to a metered commodity.

5 . The Cloud Epoch (Virtual Compute)

- **The Frontier:** In the early 2000s, if a startup wanted to build a website, they had to buy physical rack servers, rent data center space, and guess how much traffic they would get. If they guessed wrong, their site crashed or they wasted hundreds of thousands of dollars on unutilized silicon.
- **The Standardization:** In 2006, Jeff Bezos launched AWS EC2, introducing the **Instance-Hour** . Compute became a liquid utility you could turn on and off with a credit card.
- **Time it took:** **~** **6 years** for data centers to transition from rigid hardware assets to liquid cloud utilities.
