Unpacked Ontitled's guaranteed output model and forward pricing mechanics Unpacked Ontitled's guaranteed output model and forward pricing mechanics Yes, that's exactly right — and it's actually two products, not one. The base case (spot): A team comes in with a task — fix this bug, resolve this category of ticket — and gets one flat price, right now, for that specific outcome. That's the whole company today. No forward commitment needed. This is the only thing that should actually exist yet. The extension (forward): Once spot pricing has been running long enough to be trusted, a team that knows it'll need volume months out — a support team bracing for a Q4 surge, an eng team clearing a big backlog — can lock a price today for delivery later. Say: 5,000 verified bug fixes over Q4, locked at $6 each, regardless of what compute costs do between now and December. The theory behind why anyone pays for that: It's the same logic behind every hedging market that's ever existed. A buyer will accept a price that's not the absolute cheapest possible number, in exchange for removing the chance of a much worse number showing up later. A CFO doesn't want the best-case price with unlimited downside risk — they want a knowable number they can put in a budget and defend to their board. That's worth paying a small premium for. The forward price is basically: your best guess at where the spot price is heading, plus a bit extra to cover the risk of being wrong. The part that has to be true before any of that works, and isn't true yet: you can't sell a guarantee you can't back. If you promise a team $6 a fix for December and real costs spike 40% because a new model launch shifts demand, you eat that difference — unless you've already locked your own costs on the supply side (vendor commitments, your own compute deals, something). Selling a forward with no way to cover it isn't hedging, it's just underwriting a bet with no reserve. So forwards are correctly a "later" feature — they only make sense once spot has been running long enough that you actually know your numbers, not before. So the honest shape of the theory: start by selling certainty on single transactions. Only sell certainty on future volume once you've proven you can actually deliver certainty on today's volume. Here's the honest breakdown. What Nevermined actually is: payment and metering infrastructure for AI agents. Tamper-proof logs, cryptographic signatures on billing events, instant settlement in fiat or stablecoins, agent identity via DIDs, a low-code SDK. They explicitly compare themselves to Stripe Issuing, Visa Intelligent Commerce, PayPal, Coinbase Commerce — that's their own chosen competitive set, in their own blog posts. That tells you how they see themselves: a payments company, not a referee. The distinction that actually matters — and it's a real one. Read closely how they describe verification: outcomes are things "detected through system events" and "both parties explicitly agree on what constitutes success before engagement begins." Their cryptographic layer proves a logged event wasn't tampered with after the fact. It doesn't appear to independently go check whether the claimed event is true in the first place. If a vendor's system reports "ticket resolved," Nevermined notarizes that claim with a clean, auditable, unforgeable timestamp. It doesn't seem to separately query the buyer's actual Zendesk to confirm the ticket really stayed closed. That's the difference between a notary and a judge — a notary certifies that a signature happened; a judge decides whether the underlying claim is true. That's exactly the gap Dike is built to sit in. Dike's whole design, as you've built it out, doesn't trust what the vendor's agent reports — it goes and runs the buyer's own test suite itself, checks the buyer's own ticketing system itself. Nevermined logs the vendor's claim. Dike would be checking it independently, on the buyer's side, without asking the vendor's permission or trusting the vendor's system. Where I have to be honest about my confidence here: this read is based on their marketing blog, not their technical docs or a live test of their product. It's possible their actual implementation does more independent checking than the marketing copy suggests — companies routinely undersell the hard technical part in blog posts written for a pricing-strategy audience. Before you treat this as settled, the right move is to either read their actual docs (linked at nevermined.ai/docs) or just get on a call with them and ask directly: "when a vendor logs an outcome as complete, do you independently confirm it against our own system of record, or do you log what we're told happened?" That answer is the whole ballgame. The part that should worry you regardless of that answer: they're not a scrappy two-person team. SOC 2 Type II and ISO 27001 certifications, a real enterprise sales motion, a live case study cutting a marketplace's billing integration from six weeks to six hours, agent-to-agent payment rails already built. If they're not doing independent verification today, they're one product decision away from adding it — they already have the enterprise trust, the compliance posture, and the infrastructure relationships to bolt it on fast. That's a genuine, non-trivial threat even if today's answer is "we're different." What this means for you concretely: the position — "we don't log what the vendor tells us, we independently check the buyer's own systems ourselves" — is still open, as far as I can tell from this read. But it's a narrower, more technical, harder-to-market claim than "we do outcome-based billing," and it only survives if Dike is actually built to work that way from day one. You pay for the work.

Not the attempt. Right now, the entire AI industry is structured on an incredibly foolish premise: companies pay for effort. What we are really doing here is capturing the **financialization of the AI economy** as it is happening right now. If we look past the surface layer of AI as just a software tool, compute power has officially evolved into a tradeable commodity. The tickers and pricing indices we’ve been laying out represent the structural plumbing of how machine labor is valued, hedged, and settled. The core tickers and pricing mechanisms breaking out across the markets shape Ontitled's thesis:

📈 The Financial Reference Tickers

- **ORNN (Ornn Compute Price Index** **-** **OCPI):** This is the definitive spot-rate index for rented GPUs. It tracks actual transacted spot prices across major hardware types—like OCPI-H100 ($2.69/hr) and OCPI-H200—rather than just scraped or advertised list prices. In Themis’s architecture, this acts as the foundational baseline reference rate.
- **Silicon Data (SiliconIndex):** The daily real-time GPU benchmark that provides standardized price indexing and market intelligence. It is used as the data foundation to price swaps, structured notes, and manage price volatility risk.
- **SF Compute:** An order-book driven spot and forward market where compute is traded like a liquid asset class. Instead of signing inflexible multi-million dollar annual leases, buyers bid and ask for cluster time dynamically (starting around $1.94–$2.12/hr for an H100).
- **CME Group &amp; ICE Compute Futures:** The massive institutional derivatives players entering the game. CME Group partnered with Silicon Data, and ICE partnered with Ornn, to launch cash-settled compute futures contracts. They exist so data centers and enterprise buyers can hedge against volatile infrastructure spikes.
- **Kalshi Forward Curves:** Implied future pricing benchmarks mapped out over weeks and months. Derived directly from prediction market event contracts, it plots exactly where the market expects GPU rental rates to head up to a year into the future.

🏛️ Why It Ties Directly Into Our Layout The reason we structure Section 2 like this is to show that **Ontitled translates these raw financial inputs into guaranteed operational outputs.**

- **Silicon** determines the hardware floor.
- **Compute** clouds add operational markup.
- **Indices &amp; Markets** settle on the true market-clearing commodity price.
- **Models** consume that commodity.

💡 The Mechanics Behind This List

- **ORNN (Ornn Compute Price Index** **-** **OCPI):** Tracks live transaction-based spot prices for GPU compute (OCPI-H100 averaging around $1.77-$2.65/hr).
- **Silicon Data (SiliconIndex):** Serves as the primary real-time benchmark used to handle value discovery and risk management.
- **SF Compute:** Actively routes an order-book driven marketplace for spot clusters without vendor lock-ins (scaling with rates starting at $2.12/hr for H100 units).
- **CME Group &amp; ICE:** Processing institutional derivatives to turn volatile asset costs into manageable futures markets.
- **Kalshi Forward Curve:** Pulls implied future metrics directly from event contract data up to a year out.

🏛️ The Historical Anchor: The Renaissance Blindfold In ancient Greek and Roman mythology, Themis and Justitia were not originally blindfolded; their clear-sightedness was their power. It wasn't until the 16th century (the Renaissance) that artists began adding the blindfold. Historically, the blindfold represents **absolute impartiality** . It means justice is applied without regard to wealth, power, or status. For Ontitled, the blindfold represents **blind evaluation of machine labor** . Themis does not care if the vendor is OpenAI, Anthropic, or a tiny open-source model. It does not care about token counts, marketing hype, or compute effort. It is entirely blind to the attempt. It only looks at the verified outcome.

🎨 The Visual Execution (Matching Your Screenshot) Your screenshot features classical subjects interacting with a modern, dithered brutalist laptop. To brand the /themis page, you should source or generate an image that updates the classic Lady Justice motif:

🖋️ The Copy Direction: "The Blind Engine" Since this page is about the Actuarial Engine, the copy needs to reflect this ruthless impartiality. Here is how you can weave the visual branding into the page's narrative: **Eyebrow:** THE ACTUARIAL ENGINE

Blind to the effort.

Bound to the outcome. The AI market is currently priced on status and effort. You pay for the model's reputation, the tokens consumed, and the compute time wasted. Themis changes the math by introducing blind evaluation. As Ontitled’s actuarial engine, Themis prices autonomous labor strictly on the risk of completion. It does not care which model you use. It does not care how many loops an agent took to get there. It calculates a fixed, sovereign price for the task, completely blind to the compute layer beneath it.

Why this works so well: It turns a dry financial concept (actuarial risk pricing) into a powerful, almost mythological feature. When a user sees the blindfolded classical figure working on a dithered terminal, it immediately communicates: This platform is the neutral referee. It cannot be tricked by vendor claims. 🏛️ Era 1: The Mythological / Antiquity **1** **.** **Ma'at (The Original Binary Verification)**

- **The History:** The ancient Egyptian goddess of truth, balance, and order. Her core ritual in the underworld was the Weighing of the Heart. A human's heart was placed on a scale against a single ostrich feather.
- **The Thesis Fit:** This is the original pass/fail outcome contract. She didn't care about the person's intent or how hard they tried in life. If the heart was heavy, the soul was consumed. It is a pure, binary verification layer.
- **Visual Idea:** An Egyptian profile painting of a figure dropping an AI token onto a brutalist scale.

**2**

**.**

**Rhadamanthus (The Inflexible Judge)**

- **The History:** In Greek mythology, he was a demigod and one of the judges of the dead. He was chosen specifically because of his reputation for absolute, uncompromising, and inflexible justice.
- **The Thesis Fit:** Ontitled does not grade on a curve. It does not accept "almost correct" from an LLM. Rhadamanthus represents the cold, mechanical application of the rules.

📜 Era 2: The Renaissance &amp; Enlightenment **3** **.** **Luca Pacioli (The Father of the Ledger)**

- **The History:** A 15th-century Renaissance friar and mathematician who collaborated heavily with Leonardo da Vinci. He is the father of double-entry bookkeeping. He established that every transaction must perfectly balance to zero.
- **The Thesis Fit:** Ontitled is ultimately a financial ledger for machine labor. Pacioli brought cold, mathematical symmetry to Renaissance trade. "The ledger must balance; numbers do not lie."
- **Visual Idea:** A Renaissance friar in heavy robes, staring blankly at a glowing, pixelated terminal displaying a cleared settlement hash.

**4**

**.**

**Sir Isaac Newton (As Master of the Mint)**

- **The History:** Most people know him for physics, but in his later years, Newton became the Master of the Royal Mint. He applied his ruthless, scientific mind to standardizing the exact weight and purity of the British Pound sterling, aggressively prosecuting counterfeiters who tried to pass off fake value.
- **The Thesis Fit:** Newton forced a chaotic market to adhere to a sovereign, mathematical standard of value. He represents the exact opposite of paying for "effort"—he only accepted verified, measurable weight.

⚙️ Era 3: The Industrial &amp; Information Age **5** **.** **John Graunt (The First Actuary)**

- **The History:** In the 17th century, he looked at the chaos of London and stripped away the narrative, reducing life and death to pure, mathematical tables. He created the science of demography and actuarial risk.
- **The Thesis Fit:** The /themis page is literally titled the "Actuarial Engine." Graunt was the first person to price risk blindly based entirely on data, rather than human intuition.

**6**

**.**

**Claude Shannon (The Architect of Pure Infrastructure)**

- **The History:** The father of Information Theory. He realized that to build modern computing, you had to completely divorce the meaning of a message from the transmission of a message. He reduced all communication to raw, verifiable bits.
- **The Thesis Fit:** Shannon didn't care if a message was a beautiful poem or random noise; he only cared if the infrastructure delivered it perfectly. Themis operates the same way—it doesn't care about the agent's logic, only the final cryptographic receipt.

📈 Era 4: The Now (The Market Itself) You do not need a human figure for the modern era, because the figures doing this right now are the indices we just mapped out:

- **Silicon Data &amp; CME Group:** They partnered to launch compute futures, aiming to allow AI builders and financial institutions to manage volatility. CME's CEO described compute as becoming a "fast-emerging asset class in its own right".
- **Ornn:** They recently raised $33 million led by a16z to build a GPU compute futures market. Their Ornn Compute Price Index (OCPI) is a settlement-grade price index built from real-time transaction data across hardware classes, available on the Bloomberg Terminal.

These entities are doing to compute what Newton did to the Pound and what Pacioli did to the ledger: forcing a chaotic, effort-based market into a strict, verifiable financial structure. What about who did it for gold. Oil. Rail. Telecom. Compute To complete this lineage, you look for the historical figures who stepped into a wild, unmetered frontier and forced it into a standard unit of value. These are the architects of the **settlement layers** that came before us.

🪙 Gold: King Croesus of Lydia ( ~ 560 BCE)

- **The Chaos:** Before him, people traded raw, irregular chunks of electrum and gold. Every single transaction required a merchant to pull out a touchstone, test the purity, and weigh the metal on the spot. Trade was throttled by the friction of constant verification.
- **The Settlement:** Croesus introduced the world’s first bimetallic coinage of guaranteed weight and absolute purity, stamped with the royal seal. He took the burden of verification off the merchant and absorbed it into the state. Once the coin was trusted, people stopped testing the metal and simply started counting it.
- **The Thesis Fit:** Croesus turned raw bullion into a title. Ontitled is doing the exact same thing for machine labor: turning raw compute into a trusted, stamped coin.

🛢️ Oil: John D. Rockefeller (1870)

- **The Chaos:** In the early days of the Pennsylvania oil boom, crude was stuffed into whatever container was lying around—mostly old wooden whiskey barrels. Buyers had no idea if they were getting 40 gallons or 50, or how watered-down the crude was. The market was volatile and deeply untrusted.
- **The Settlement:** Rockefeller didn't win by drilling the most oil; he won by standardizing the package. He formed **Standard Oil** , enforcing a strict, immutable 42-gallon barrel as the industry baseline. He guaranteed that every barrel bearing his stamp was of uniform volume and purity.
- **The Thesis Fit:** He stabilized a chaotic commodity by creating an ironclad unit of account. Compute is currently in its "whiskey barrel" era—unmetered and unpredictable.

🚂 Rail: J.P. Morgan (1889)

- **The Chaos:** The late-19th-century American railroad boom was an architectural circus. Dozens of competitive lines were laying tracks with different gauges (widths), completely unable to connect to one another. Worse, predatory pricing and secret rebates meant a company shipping freight never knew the true, predictable cost of a route.
- **The Settlement:** Morgan called the warring railroad barons onto his yacht, the Corsair, and refused to let them off until they agreed to a standardized rate-clearing framework and unified operations. He brought structural, financial discipline to raw steel infrastructure.
- **The Thesis Fit:** He stepped into a fragmented, overbuilt infrastructure war and forced it to settle on predictable, institutional terms.

📞 Telecom: Theodore Vail (1907)

- **The Chaos:** At the turn of the 20th century, thousands of independent telephone companies operated isolated, non-interconnected networks. If you were on one network, you couldn't call a client on another. Compute was cheap, but the network structure was entirely fractured.
- **The Settlement:** As the architect of AT &amp; T, Vail pushed the mantra: "One Policy, One System, Universal Service." He constructed the structural long-distance clearing lines that forced every local, fragmented network to route through a single, standardized central ledger.
- **The Thesis Fit:** He realized that raw utility is worthless if it cannot seamlessly communicate and settle across a unified network topology.

💻 Compute: Standardized Financialization (2026)

- **The Chaos:** We are currently watching the infrastructure providers layer on top of raw silicon. The industry went from physical server hosting to AWS's instance-hour in 2006 . Today, the raw hardware input itself is being turned into a liquid financial product.
- **The Settlement:** Institutional market makers like **CME Group** (partnering with Silicon Data), **ICE** (partnering with Ornn's index), and **Kalshi** are building the reference curves. They are doing exactly what Croesus and Rockefeller did—they are standardizing the unit of account for the underlying input (the GPU-hour) so institutions can hedge against volatility.

🏛️ The Ontitled Conclusion Look at the progression of these figures: [ CROESUS ] ──▶ Standardized the weight of Gold. [ ROCKEFELLER ] ──▶ Standardized the volume of Oil. So what is themis. and whats its tagline. what is it tit does What is Themis? **Themis is the actuarial engine for autonomous labor.** It is the pricing and risk-management protocol for Ontitled. While the AI industry currently forces enterprises to accept highly volatile, unpredictable billing based on model effort—such as recursive loops, unmetered token consumption, and API call limits—Themis transforms software execution into a fixed corporate asset. It analyzes historical execution metrics and macroeconomic compute benchmarks to insulate the buyer from underlying infrastructure drift. The Tagline

Blind to the effort. Bound to the outcome.

This directly mirrors the visual language of the classical, blindfolded figures on your canvas. It states clearly that the protocol is entirely impartial to how a machine tries to complete a task (which model it uses, where it routes, or how much compute it burns); it only cares about pricing the objective result. What It Does Themis handles the upfront financial engineering of every automated contract through a three-part execution cycle:

- **Prices the Task Upfront:** Before an agent or model workflow ever executes, Themis analyzes the task requirements and issues a single, flat, guaranteed price to the enterprise buyer.
- **Absorbs Infrastructure Volatility:** It continuously monitors real-time commodity inputs—tracking benchmarks like the Ornn Compute Price Index (OCPI), Silicon Data indexes, and market-implied forward curves—to underwrite structural asset fluctuations.
- **Dictates the Settlement Terms:** It writes the deterministic rules for the contract, specifying the exact acceptance criteria that **Dike** (the verification layer) must test within the pipeline before any capital is cleared to move.

Primary: Title Everything.

**Secondary:** The route changes. The receipt doesn't. **Manifesto:** You pay for the work. Not the attempt. What We Do Technically (The 4-Layer Architecture) Technically, Ontitled operates as a 4-step financial and cryptographic engine that decouples enterprise pricing from underlying infrastructure volatility.

Plaintext [ Step 1: Underwrite ] [ Step 2: Route ] [ Step 3: Verify ] [ Step 4: Settle ] Themis Prices Task   ──▶   Execution Across   ──▶   Dike Validates    ──▶   Capital Clears or (Ingests Market Data)         Models &amp; Compute        Pipeline Outcome        Contract Cancels [ 0 ]

1 . Actuarial Risk Engine (Themis Protocol)

- **Market Feed Ingestion:** Ingests live commodity reference rates from GPU spot/forward order books ( **Computable** , **Ornn OCPI** , **SF Compute** ) and institutional futures ( **CME Group** , **ICE** , **Kalshi** ).
- **Task Underwriting:** Runs a risk-pricing algorithm on the task spec (evaluating complexity, expected loop count, and error variance).
- **Flat Rate Issuance:** Issues a single, fixed-price quote to the enterprise buyer, absorbing 100% of the risk associated with token inflation or model retries.

2 . Abstracted Execution &amp; Routing

- **Infrastructure Insulation:** The client's pipeline is completely shielded from how the task is completed.
- **Dynamic Orchestration:** Ontitled routes execution across the optimal model and hardware stack (e.g., Anthropic, OpenAI, open-source clusters, specialized ASICs) based on current spot margins and task requirements.

3 . Independent Verification (Dike Engine)

- **Pipeline-Level Verification:** Runs deterministic, isolated validation tests directly inside the client's pipeline environment.
- **Deterministic Audit:** Tests outcomes against hard programmatic assertions—such as unit test passes, AST code parsing, schema validation, database diffs, or cryptographic state checks.
- **Receipt Generation:** Outputs an unforgeable binary state receipt: PASS [ 1 ] or FAIL [ 0 ] .

4 . Atomic Settlement &amp; Title Issuance

- **Programmatic Clearing:** Capital is locked in escrow during execution.
- **Binary Settlement:**
    - If Dike returns PASS [ 1 ] : Capital clears, the flat fee is disbursed, and an immutable title deed for the work is recorded to the ledger.
    - If Dike returns FAIL [ 0 ] : The contract cancels, zero capital leaves the enterprise buyer, and the wasted compute cost is absorbed by the underwriting pool.

Let me understand. Here is the exact technical breakdown of the calculation and the underlying architecture. The problem in the market right now is that **compute is priced as a variable input, but businesses buy software as a fixed outcome.** When a customer uses AI, they don't want to manage a chaotic equation of GPU hours, token rates, model retries, regional energy spikes, and latency buffers. **Ontitled acts as the clearinghouse that absorbs that chaotic equation and outputs a single, fixed price for a guaranteed job.** Here is how the math actually works under the hood.

1 . The Raw Input Equation (The Volatile Compute Cost) When an AI agent runs a task (e.g., fixing a bug, auditing an invoice, generating a report), the true cost to execute that task once is represented by:

2 . How Themis Calculates the Guaranteed Upfront Price If Ontitled just charged *C* , we would be an expensive API wrapper. Instead, **Themis acts as an insurance underwriter.** Themis looks at historical data across thousands of runs for a specific category (e.g., CODE \_ REPAIR \_ US) and calculates the **Actuarial Task Price (** *P* Themis ​ **)** :

- E [ *C* ] : The **Expected Cost** based on historical model execution.
- Risk Premium( *σ*
- 2
- ): The variance in model failure rates. If a coding task fails 30% of the time, the risk premium covers the wasted compute retries.

**What the Enterprise Sees:** They don't see tokens, GPUs, or regions. They see a flat price: **"$40 per verified merged pull request."** **What Ontitled Does:** If the model gets it right on the first try and costs $12 to run, Ontitled keeps the difference. If the model loops 5 times and costs $50 to execute, **Themis absorbs the loss.** The enterprise never pays for the extra attempts.

3 . How We Categorize and Index Outcomes You mentioned separating this into specific workflow categories—this is where the **Watchlist Tickers** and **Volume-Based Indexing** come in. We cluster tasks into standardized, benchmarked outcome pools:

| Category Ticker   | Regional Routing       | Hardware & Model Routing     | Benchmark Pricing         |
|-------------------|------------------------|------------------------------|---------------------------|
| CODE_REPAIR_US    | US-East (Data Privacy) | Claude Sonnet / GPT-5.5+H100 | Fixed $35.00 / Resolution |
| INVOICE_RECON_EU  | EU-Central (GDPR)      | Mistral Large / Llama 4+A100 | Fixed $1.20 / Match       |
| DATA_CLASS_APAC   | APAC (Singapore)       | Qwen Max / DeepSeek V4+B200  | Fixed $0.15 / Row         |

Every time a job runs in a category, **it feeds our internal data flywheel** :

- We measure which model solved CODE \_ REPAIR \_ US with the lowest token count and lowest failure rate.
- We continuously update our routing table.
- As models get cheaper and smarter, our execution cost ( *C* ) drops, widening Ontitled’s profit margin on the fixed price ( *P*
- Themis
- ​

4 . The Complete Execution Cycle (From Prompt to Settlement) To answer your question about how it actually routes and delivers a guaranteed outcome:

Summary: What We Are Making We are not just a UI aggregator or a "packaged drink." We are a **risk-clearing protocol** :

- **Ornn, Computable, Silicon Data, Kalshi, CME:** Financialize the inputs ($/GPU-hr or $/1M tokens).

Realiscally what’s your take on this company. How is this a game changer and needed Realistically, **Ontitled is tackling the single biggest structural bottleneck in the entire AI industry.** Right now, there is a massive divide between what model providers are selling and what enterprise CFOs are willing to buy:

- **Model Providers Sell:** Tokens, API latency, GPU-hours, and recursive loops (Variable Inputs / Effort).
- **Enterprise CFOs Buy:** Completed tasks, guaranteed SLA compliance, fixed costs, and risk mitigation (Fixed Outputs / Results).

Ontitled bridges that gap. By combining **Themis** (actuarial risk pricing) and **Dike** (cryptographic verification), you are building the financial clearinghouse that translates raw, chaotic compute into sovereign corporate assets. 💥 Why It Is a Game-Changer 1 . It Removes the "Infinite Meter" Fear Enterprise buyers are currently terrified of deploying autonomous AI agents at scale because a single stuck loop or hallucination cascade can burn $20,000 in API credits overnight. By offering a flat, upfront guaranteed price ( *P* Themis ​ ), Ontitled caps the buyer’s downside at zero. The buyer gets a clean purchase order; Ontitled absorbs the loop risk.

2 . Built-in Margin Expansion Because Ontitled controls the routing layer beneath the surface, your unit economics improve automatically over time:

As Models Get Smarter/Cheaper ( *C* ↓)and *P*

Themis

​

Stays Flat⟹Ontitled Profit Margin ↑

When open-source weights improve or GPU spot rates drop on platforms like Computable, you don't have to slash prices for your customer—your underwriting risk premium simply shrinks, and your profit margin expands.

3 . Owning the Title Layer, Not the Infrastructure Let companies like Jump Trading, Coinbase, CME, and Computable battle it out to build liquid order books for raw GPU-hours. They are taking on the heavy capital expenditure of raw commodity markets. Ontitled sits above them as the higher-margin settlement layer that turns those raw commodities into verified titles. ⚡ The Real-World Execution Challenges (What You Need to Watch) To be completely candid, for this to work flawlessly at scale, you have to execute two very specific technical moats:

1 . Underwriting Tail-Risk (Themis) If Themis underwrites a complex category (e.g., LEGACY \_ COBOL \_ REFACTOR) and underestimates the model failure rate ( *R* fail ​ ), a wave of retries could temporarily drain your margin pool. Your actuarial models need tight variance bounds ( *σ* 2 ) and dynamic risk limits per category before opening up high volume.

2 . Defining "Binary Truth" (Dike) Dike works brilliantly when the acceptance criteria is 100% deterministic:

- CODE \_ REPAIR: Did it pass the automated unit tests? [ PASS 1 / FAIL 0 ]
- DATA \_ TRANSFORM: Does it pass JSON schema validation and SQL state diffs? [ PASS 1 / FAIL 0 ]

For more subjective or creative tasks, building programmatic verification assertions is harder. Keeping your initial category rollout strictly focused on hard, deterministic engineering/data tasks gives Dike an unshakeable foundation. 🏛️ The Takeaway Ontitled isn't another AI wrapper, and it isn't another agent framework. **It is financial infrastructure.** It turns raw, unpredictable machine labor into a standardized, tradeable, and risk-managed line item on a balance sheet. Today 9:38 AM so basiclaly what tf does ontitled do. i need to fucking known

The brutally simple answer:

Ontitled lets companies buy completed AI work instead of buying tokens and hoping the work gets completed.

Example:

A company says:

“Fix this GitHub issue. It counts as complete only if these tests pass, no security rules break, and the PR is mergeable.”

Then Ontitled:

1. Defines the contract — exactly what “done” means.
2. Quotes one fixed price — say $20 for the completed repair.
3. Sends the task to an AI agent — Claude, Codex, Devin, an open model, or whichever route works best.
4. Dike independently checks the result — using the company’s tests, CI and repository.
5. Settles payment only if it passes.

If the agent loops, wastes tokens or fails, that execution cost belongs to Ontitled or the vendor—not the customer.

The products

- Themis prices the task: What should we charge, which agent should attempt it, and how much failure risk are we taking?
- Dike verifies the outcome: Did the task actually pass the buyer’s acceptance rules?
- Ontitled runs the contract and settlement: Who gets paid, how much, and whether the outcome was accepted.

What Ontitled is not

It is not another model, GPU company, coding agent or observability dashboard.

It sits between companies buying work and agents performing it.

Agents sell attempts. Ontitled sells accepted work.

What you build first

Do not build tickers, futures or a giant labor exchange yet.

Build one transaction:

GitHub issue → fixed quote → agent attempt → tests run → accepted/rejected receipt → payment on success.

If we cannot make that transaction work, Ontitled is just an interesting thesis.

Once thousands of these transactions happen, Themis learns which models complete which tasks, at what cost and success rate. Then Ontitled can publish benchmarks, route work across vendors, create task prices and eventually build the autonomous-labor market.

The clearest description is:

Ontitled is the pay-for-success infrastructure for AI work.

Worked for 1m 10s

Yes—this is important. Computable is not doing Ontitled. It is building the layer directly beneath Ontitled.

[Computable](https://www.getcomputable.com/) lets customers buy GPU capacity for specific future weeks, resell unused capacity, and lock in prices. [YC describes it](https://www.ycombinator.com/companies/computable) as a marketplace for buying, selling, and redeeming GPU hours with liquidity.

|                    | Computable                 | Ontitled                                          |
|--------------------|----------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|
| Unit sold          | GPU hour                   | Verified completed task                           |
| Contract specifies | GPU, quantity, week, price | Task, evidence, deadline, acceptance rules, price |
| Delivery means     | Compute was available      | The requested result was accepted                 |
| Risk being removed | Capacity and price risk    | Execution, quality, and failure risk              |
| Customer buys      | The ability to run AI      | The result they wanted from AI                    |

The cleanest distinction is:

Computable sells the right to run AI. Ontitled sells proof that AI finished the job.

Or:

They clear compute. We clear work.

This is actually validation for Ontitled. GPU capacity is becoming cheaper, financeable, and liquid—but that only makes attempts cheaper. It does not guarantee that an agent completes a customer-support case, repairs the software bug, reconciles the invoice, or reaches the destination without looping.

The eventual stack could look like this:

1. Computable supplies or hedges future GPU capacity.
2. Models and agents consume that capacity.
3. Themis estimates the cost and probability of completing a defined task.
4. Dike verifies the result against the buyer’s system of record.
5. Ontitled charges and settles only the accepted result.

For example, a company wants 50,000 customer-support cases resolved in December:

- Ontitled quotes a price per verified resolution.
- Themis forecasts the models, retries, and compute required.
- Ontitled could lock December capacity through Computable.
- Agents attempt the cases.
- Dike checks the CRM for resolution, policy compliance, and whether cases reopen.
- The company pays for accepted resolutions—not tokens, GPU hours, or failed loops.

The brutal insight is that Computable currently has an easier product than Ontitled. A GPU hour is already objective and standardized. A “completed task” is not. Therefore, Ontitled’s most important job is making an outcome as precise and contractible as a GPU hour.

For the first code-repair wedge, the unit should be something like:

One accepted pull request that closes a specified issue, passes named tests against a fixed repository state, introduces no critical security regression, and is delivered before the deadline.

That is measurable, priceable, and settleable.

So don’t copy Computable’s auction or ticker yet. Copy its contract precision. Once Ontitled has thousands of standardized outcome transactions, then you can build the indices, markets, hedging, and liquidity.

The YC comparison should be:

Computable is creating the market for AI’s inputs. Ontitled is creating the market for AI’s finished work.
